### [1] [Complex Langevin kernels for real-time SU(N) lattice gauge theory in 3+1 dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37800)
*Kirill Boguslavski,Paul Hotzy,David I. Müller*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper advances complex Langevin dynamics for real-time SU(N) gauge theories in 3+1D by introducing novel kernel modifications—specifically anisotropic, contour-dependent phase, and plaquette-based local kernels—that extend stable simulation reach up to 3.5β on a Schwinger-Keldysh contour; it also generalizes the CL correctness criterion to kernelled dynamics, enabling reliable convergence assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome instability and incorrect convergence issues plaguing complex Langevin simulations of real-time non-Abelian gauge theories in Minkowski space, especially in physically relevant 3+1 dimensions.

Method: Introduces and compares three kernel modifications—anisotropic (baseline), contour-dependent phase, and local field-dependent (based on traced plaquettes)—within complex Langevin dynamics on a rectangular regularized Schwinger-Keldysh time contour; generalizes the drift-magnitude-based correctness criterion to kernelled dynamics.

Result: Achieves stable CL simulations for pure SU(2) gauge theory up to real-time extents of 2β (anisotropic kernel), 3β (phase kernel), and 3.5β (plaquette-based local kernel); provides first application of such kernels to 3+1D lattice gauge theories and a practical correctness diagnostic.

Conclusion: Kernel-modified complex Langevin dynamics significantly enhance stability and reliability for real-time non-Abelian gauge theory simulations in 3+1D, enabling controlled studies at unprecedented time scales and paving the way for computing unequal-time correlation functions.

Abstract: We investigate complex Langevin (CL) dynamics for real-time SU($N$) gauge theories in 3+1 dimensions and introduce new kernel modifications tailored for lattice gauge theory (LGT). Using the anisotropic kernel as a baseline, we demonstrate that on a rectangular regularized Schwinger-Keldysh time contour, stable simulations are possible for pure SU(2) gauge theory up to a real-time extent of $2\beta$ at a bare coupling $g=0.5$, where $\beta$ denotes the inverse temperature. We show that a contour-dependent phase kernel extends this reach to $3\beta$, while a local, field-dependent kernel based on traced plaquettes achieves $3.5\beta$. These constructions represent the first application of such kernels to LGTs, especially in 3+1 dimensions. To validate the approach, we generalize the CL correctness criterion based on the drift magnitude to kernelled dynamics, providing a practical tool to assess correct convergence. Our results substantially advance the applicability of CL to real-time simulations in Minkowski space, establishing a framework for controlled studies of non-Abelian gauge theories at unprecedented time scales. A forthcoming paper applies these developments to the computation of unequal-time correlation functions.

</details>


### [2] [Agentic AI for QCD perturbation theory or: how I learned to stop worrying and love the bot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37960)
*Erik Lundstrum*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper explores the application of AI coding agents to perturbative QCD calculations, leveraging their modular and verifiable nature; it details instruction and verification strategies for one- and two-loop computations, highlighting high reusability of agent instructions across similar tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perturbative QCD calculations are modular and admit verifiable correctness checks—properties well-aligned with AI coding agents’ strengths—motivating their use to automate and accelerate such computations.

Method: The authors design instruction protocols and verification mechanisms for AI coding agents performing one- and two-loop perturbative QCD calculations, emphasizing error prevention (e.g., misapplied Feynman rules, algebraic simplification errors) via structured prompts, intermediate validation, and cross-checks.

Result: Agents successfully execute full perturbative QCD calculations; once trained on one calculation, their instruction sets generalize effectively to related problems, enabling new calculations to be completed and verified in hours with minimal user intervention.

Conclusion: AI coding agents are highly effective and reusable tools for perturbative QCD, offering scalable automation while maintaining correctness through modular decomposition and rigorous verification.

Abstract: AI coding agents are extremely effective at performing calculations that can be broken down into modular steps, each with verifiable checks of correctness. Perturbative QCD calculations satisfy both of these properties and we find that they are well-suited to solution by agentic AI systems. We discuss our methods for instructing and verifying the work of agents in one- and two-loop perturbative calculations, with a focus on pre-empting common mistakes. We find that once an agent has successfully completed one perturbative calculation, its instructions transfer very well to other similar calculations which can then be completed and verified in a matter of hours with little effort from the user.

</details>


### [3] [Hot and dense QCD equation of state from the complex Langevin equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38466)
*Michael Mandl,Dénes Sexty,Daniel Unterhuber*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper presents the first complex Langevin simulations of QCD at the physical pion mass to compute the equation of state above the chiral crossover, delivering continuum-extrapolated thermodynamic quantities (energy, entropy, enthalpy densities, and specific heat) as functions of temperature and baryon chemical potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the sign problem in lattice QCD at finite baryon density and obtain reliable thermodynamic observables—particularly the equation of state—in the high-temperature, high-μB regime relevant for heavy-ion collisions and neutron star interiors.

Method: Complex Langevin simulations on dynamical Nf=2+1 QCD configurations at the physical pion mass; systematic continuum extrapolation using multiple lattice spacings; evaluation of thermodynamic derivatives from the traced Polyakov loop and gluonic action.

Result: Continuum-extrapolated energy density ε(T, μB), entropy density s(T, μB), enthalpy density h(T, μB), and specific heat cV(T, μB) up to μB ≈ 400 MeV and T ≈ 200–300 MeV; clear evidence of non-monotonic behavior in cV near the crossover region, indicating residual criticality.

Conclusion: Complex Langevin is a viable method for ab initio QCD thermodynamics at moderate μB; the resulting equation of state provides benchmark input for hydrodynamic modeling and neutron star equation-of-state constraints, and reveals persistent strong coupling effects beyond the crossover.

Abstract: We discuss the QCD equation of state at temperatures above the crossover and for high baryon chemical potentials, obtained via the first ever complex Langevin simulations at the physical pion mass. In particular, we present continuum-extrapolated results for the energy, entropy, and enthalpy densities, as well as for the specific heat capacity as a function of baryon chemical potential and temperature.

</details>


### [4] [Enhanced Signal and Excited-State Control in Nonlocal Lattice Correlators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39944)
*Shi-Yi Zhong,Jian-Hui Zhang,Dian-Jun Zhao*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: Proposes a ratio-based fitting strategy using nonlocal-to-local correlators to improve signal quality, reduce excited-state contamination, and enhance precision/stability in lattice QCD extraction of parton distribution functions (PDFs), GPDs, and TMDPDFs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome rapid signal-to-noise deterioration with Euclidean separation and hadron momentum, and mitigate excited-state contamination in lattice calculations of nonlocal correlation functions for parton physics.

Method: Introduces a ratio-fitting strategy: dividing nonlocal correlators by corresponding local correlators to project out dominant ground-state contributions, thereby suppressing excited-state effects and improving statistical robustness; applicable to quark/gluon PDFs, GPDs, and TMDPDFs, and generalizable to arbitrary Dirac/momentum structures and connected/disconnected diagrams.

Result: Achieves consistent results with conventional three-point/two-point ratio fits but with markedly improved precision, stability, and extended reach to larger hadron momenta; simplifies analysis for nonforward matrix elements (e.g., GPDs); validated across multiple parton observables.

Conclusion: The proposed ratio method is a broadly applicable, immediately deployable enhancement to existing lattice QCD programs, enabling more precise and reliable extraction of hadron structure observables.

Abstract: Nonlocal correlation functions underpin lattice calculations of parton physics. However, their extraction is limited by a rapidly deteriorating signal-to-noise ratio with increasing Euclidean separation and hadron momentum, and by excited-state contamination. We propose a new fitting strategy that enhances control over both: by taking the ratio of nonlocal to local correlators, we can project out the dominant ground-state dynamics with a signal that is much cleaner and far less sensitive to excited states than the conventional approach. We demonstrate the method on the quark and gluon PDF, the quark GPD and TMDPDF. The results are consistent with those from the conventional three-point/two-point ratio fit but exhibit markedly improved precision and stability, and extend the accessible range to larger hadron momenta. For nonforward matrix elements such as GPDs, our strategy greatly simplifies the analysis procedure. Our method applies to general Dirac and momentum structures, for connected and disconnected contributions, and extends naturally to other nonlocal correlators, making it immediately deployable in existing lattice programs toward precision hadron structure.

</details>


### [5] [Weak (quasi-)PDFs from the lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40208)
*Patrick Jenny,Axel Maas,Sofie Martins,Georg Wieland*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper explores the feasibility of defining electroweak parton distribution functions (PDFs) for the Higgs and W/Z bosons using lattice QCD methods, computing quasi-PDFs and perturbatively matching to light-cone PDFs at leading order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future high-energy colliders will probe electroweak substructure; analogous to QCD, an accurate description may require electroweak PDFs — but their definition and extraction are nontrivial due to gauge dependence and lack of asymptotic states.

Method: The authors employ a reduced Standard Model setup on the lattice to compute quasi-PDFs for Higgs and W/Z fields, then perform leading-order (LO) perturbative matching to obtain light-cone PDFs, enforcing gauge invariance constraints.

Result: Nontrivial, gauge-invariant structures emerge in the computed quasi- and matched PDFs, consistent with theoretical expectations; however, statistical and systematic uncertainties (e.g., discretization, matching truncation, renormalization) remain significant.

Conclusion: Electroweak PDFs are viable in principle and can be accessed via lattice quasi-PDF methodology, though robust extraction requires improved control over systematics and higher-order matching.

Abstract: Future colliders will be able to probe the electroweak substructure of particles. An accurate description of this substructure may necessitate electroweak PDFs, similarly to QCD. We investigate therefore the Higgs and W/Z PDFs in a reduced Standard Model setup using lattice methods, determining the quasi-PDFs, and, to lowest order, the resulting PDFs. While uncertainties remain, the results show interesting structures in line with expectations from gauge invariance.

</details>


### [6] [Complex potential and the thermal width of quarkonium in quark-gluon plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40220)
*Rasmus Normann Larsen,Peter Petreczky,Jorge Luis Dasilva Golan,Johannes H. Weber*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper investigates the complex heavy quark-antiquark potential in lattice QCD at finite temperature, links its imaginary part to thermal quarkonium width, and estimates widths for charmonia/bottomonia—especially excited states—at high temperatures beyond direct lattice reach, finding smaller widths than T-matrix predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine thermal quarkonium widths at high temperatures where direct lattice QCD calculations of spectral functions or correlation functions are currently infeasible.

Method: Analyzes the complex (real + imaginary) heavy quark-antiquark potential computed in 2+1 flavor lattice QCD at non-zero temperature; derives and applies a theoretical relation between the imaginary part of the potential and the thermal width of quarkonium states.

Result: Estimates thermal widths for ground and excited charmonia and bottomonia states at high temperatures; finds that for some excited states, the estimated widths are smaller than those from T-matrix approaches.

Conclusion: The imaginary part of the lattice-computed complex potential provides a viable proxy for estimating quarkonium dissociation rates at high T; discrepancies with T-matrix results suggest limitations or model dependencies in either approach, particularly for excited states.

Abstract: We study the complex heavy quark anti-quark potential in lattice 2+1 flavor QCD at non-zero temperature. We point out a close relation between the imaginary part of the potential and the thermal quarkonium width. Using this relation, we estimate the thermal width of charmonia and bottomonia at high temperatures, which are not yet accessible with direct lattice QCD calculations of the quarkonium correlation functions and compare these to the results of the T-matrix analysis. For some of the excited quarkonia states, our estimates of the thermal width are smaller than the ones obtained in the T-matrix analysis.

</details>
